Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hi, yes, thank you for seeing me. I know I probably should have come in sooner, but you know how it is, especially working in healthcare administration, you just kind of keep pushing through the days. I’m a medical services manager for a rather large clinic, so my schedule is just... well, it's always back-to-back. 

But what finally pushed me to actually make this appointment—and honestly, it's been building up for a while now—is that I am just hitting an absolute wall with my focus at work. I can sit down at my desk to start an EPIC quality report, which is something I have to do all the time, or I'll go sit in one of our weekly clinic metrics meetings with the department heads. And I go in with every intention of tracking the conversation and doing what I need to do. I really do. I'll have my pen ready and my laptop open. But then, within just a few minutes, my mind has drifted so far away from the room or the screen. 

I’ll suddenly snap back and realize I’ve been staring at the exact same column of patient data, just rereading the same numbers over and over again without actually processing a single thing. Or worse, I'll be in that metrics meeting and someone will pause, maybe waiting for my input on the workflow, and I realize I completely missed the whole point of the discussion. My brain was just... somewhere else entirely. It’s incredibly frustrating, and frankly, it's embarrassing at my age and in my position to be sitting there realizing you haven't heard a word anyone said for the last ten minutes. I just keep trying to force myself to pay attention, but it feels like no matter how hard I try to lock in, I just drift right back out.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, it's definitely not just a recent thing. Not at all. If I really think back on it, and I have been thinking about this a lot lately since things at the clinic have gotten so bad... it’s always been there. Even way back in elementary school. I actually found some of my old report cards recently when I was helping my parents clean out their attic—which, by the way, was a whole other ordeal of me getting distracted by every old photo album I found—but anyway, I looked at the comments from my teachers. Every single year, it was the exact same story. "She's a bright girl, very capable, but she's just so careless." Or, "She needs to apply herself more." 

And the homework... oh my goodness, the homework was a nightmare. My poor mother. She practically had to tether herself to the kitchen table with me every single night just to make sure I actually finished anything. If she didn't sit right there, I would just... I don't know, start doodling, or looking out the window, or I’d suddenly decide I needed to reorganize my entire pencil case. And that was if I even remembered to bring the homework home in the first place! Half the time I would leave my math book in my desk at school, or I’d lose the assignment sheet somewhere between the classroom and the bus. I was constantly losing track of things. So yes, it’s always been a struggle, it just feels like the stakes are so much higher now with my job, you know? Back then it was just a forgotten worksheet, but now it's patient data and clinic metrics, and I just can't afford to be that same careless little girl anymore.

You know, it's funny you ask that, because my husband actually asked me something very similar just the other day. He was wondering if I was getting burned out, or maybe even depressed, because he sees how frustrated I get when I come home from the clinic. But honestly? No, not at all. I really don't feel sad or hopeless. 

I mean, don't get me wrong, I get stressed. Anyone in healthcare administration right now is stressed out of their minds. Just last week we had this huge unexpected compliance audit and I was running around trying to pull records, but that's just normal job stress, you know? It's not a general, lingering worry. I don't sit around feeling nervous about life or having dread about the future. I actually really love my life. My husband and I have a great setup, the kids are older and doing well, we actually just finished renovating our kitchen which has been a wonderful distraction... so there's really nothing for me to be genuinely down about. 

The absolutely *only* time I get upset or anxious is when it is directly tied to me dropping the ball at work. Like when I realize I've spent three hours staring at that EPIC dashboard and got absolutely nothing done, *then* I get mad at myself. I get very frustrated, and I'll definitely vent to my husband about how incompetent I feel in those specific moments. But it's entirely tied to my inability to just do my work. Once I step away from the desk, or once it's the weekend, I'm perfectly fine. It's not this dark cloud following me around, and it's not a generalized panic. It's just... sheer, utter annoyance at my own brain, if that makes sense? Because I know I am smart enough to do the job, I just can't seem to make my brain cooperate. So, yeah, no persistent sadness or anything like that. Just the focus thing.

I don't know, start doodling, or looking out the window, or I’d suddenly decide I needed to reorganize my entire pencil case. And that was if I even remembered to bring the homework home in the first place! Half the time I would leave my math book in my desk at school, or I’d lose the assignment sheet somewhere between the classroom and the bus. I was constantly losing track of things. So yes, it’s always been a struggle, it just feels like the stakes are so much higher now with my job, you know? Back then it was just a forgotten worksheet, but now it's patient data and clinic metrics, and I just can't afford to be that same careless little girl anymore.\n\nDoctor: Over the past several months, have you noticed persistent feelings of sadness, hopelessness, or excessive worry and nervousness that occur separately from these attention difficulties?\n\nPatient: You know, it's funny you ask that, because my husband actually asked me something very similar just the other day. He was wondering if I was getting burned out, or maybe even depressed, because he sees how frustrated I get when I come home from the clinic. But honestly? No, not at all. I really don't feel sad or hopeless. \n\nI mean, don't get me wrong, I get stressed. Anyone in healthcare administration right now is stressed out of their minds. Just last week we had this huge unexpected compliance audit and I was running around trying to pull records, but that's just normal job stress, you know? It's not a general, lingering worry. I don't sit around feeling nervous about life or having dread about the future. I actually really love my life. My husband and I have a great setup, the kids are older and doing well, we actually just finished renovating our kitchen which has been a wonderful distraction... so there's really nothing for me to be genuinely down about. \n\nThe absolutely *only* time I get upset or anxious is when it is directly tied to me dropping the ball at work. Like when I realize I've spent three hours staring at that EPIC dashboard and got absolutely nothing done, *then* I get mad at myself. I get very frustrated, and I'll definitely vent to my husband about how incompetent I feel in those specific moments. But it's entirely tied to my inability to just do my work. Once I step away from the desk, or once it's the weekend, I'm perfectly fine. It's not this dark cloud following me around, and it's not a generalized panic. It's just... sheer, utter annoyance at my own brain, if that makes sense? Because I know I am smart enough to do the job, I just can't seem to make my brain cooperate. So, yeah, no persistent sadness or anything like that. Just the focus thing.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, not really. I mean, I don't... I really don't have that physical restlessness at all. Actually, it’s quite the opposite. I can sit perfectly still for hours on end. My husband jokes that I turn into a statue when I’m at my computer. I’ll just sit there, completely planted in my office chair, just staring blankly at the monitor. I don't bounce my leg, I don't tap my pen, nothing like that. 

And as for interrupting people or acting impulsively, definitely not. In those metrics meetings I was telling you about, I am almost always the quietest person in the room. I would never just talk over someone. If anything, I'm probably too quiet, mostly because, like I said, my mind has usually wandered off and I’m just scrambling internally to piece together what the conversation is even about so I don't sound completely out of the loop if someone actually does ask for my opinion. 

And making quick decisions? Oh, goodness, no. I am a chronic over-thinker. I mean, working in healthcare administration, you really can't make snap decisions anyway—everything has to go through compliance, or HR, or a committee review, so I'm very used to a slow process. But even in my personal life, I take absolutely forever to decide on anything. When we were doing that kitchen renovation I brought up earlier, it literally took me three entire weeks just to pick out the cabinet hardware. Three weeks! Because I had to compare every single brushed nickel drawer pull on the internet, reading reviews, ordering little samples to hold up against the wood... so no, impulsive is probably the very last word anyone would ever use to describe me. 

It really is just the mental drift. It’s like my body is perfectly anchored to the desk, but my brain has completely left the building. Which is almost a double-edged sword, you know? Because my colleagues look at me sitting there so quietly and still, and they just assume I'm fully engaged and listening intently to every word of the presentation, when in reality I'm a million miles away thinking about grocery lists or an email I forgot to send yesterday. So, no, none of the fidgeting or impulsivity. Just the... the absolute wall with my focus.

So yes, it’s always been a struggle, it just feels like the stakes are so much higher now with my job, you know? Back then it was just a forgotten worksheet, but now it's patient data and clinic metrics, and I just can't afford to be that same careless little girl anymore.\n\nDoctor: Over the past several months, have you noticed persistent feelings of sadness, hopelessness, or excessive worry and nervousness that occur separately from these attention difficulties?\n\nPatient: You know, it's funny you ask that, because my husband actually asked me something very similar just the other day. He was wondering if I was getting burned out, or maybe even depressed, because he sees how frustrated I get when I come home from the clinic. But honestly? No, not at all. I really don't feel sad or hopeless. \n\nI mean, don't get me wrong, I get stressed. Anyone in healthcare administration right now is stressed out of their minds. Just last week we had this huge unexpected compliance audit and I was running around trying to pull records, but that's just normal job stress, you know? It's not a general, lingering worry. I don't sit around feeling nervous about life or having dread about the future. I actually really love my life. My husband and I have a great setup, the kids are older and doing well, we actually just finished renovating our kitchen which has been a wonderful distraction... so there's really nothing for me to be genuinely down about. \n\nThe absolutely *only* time I get upset or anxious is when it is directly tied to me dropping the ball at work. Like when I realize I've spent three hours staring at that EPIC dashboard and got absolutely nothing done, *then* I get mad at myself. I get very frustrated, and I'll definitely vent to my husband about how incompetent I feel in those specific moments. But it's entirely tied to my inability to just do my work. Once I step away from the desk, or once it's the weekend, I'm perfectly fine. It's not this dark cloud following me around, and it's not a generalized panic. It's just... sheer, utter annoyance at my own brain, if that makes sense? Because I know I am smart enough to do the job, I just can't seem to make my brain cooperate. So, yeah, no persistent sadness or anything like that. Just the focus thing.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the attention difficulties, do you notice things like feeling restless, fidgeting, having trouble sitting still, or acting impulsively—like interrupting others or making quick decisions without thinking them through?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I don't... I really don't have that physical restlessness at all. Actually, it’s quite the opposite. I can sit perfectly still for hours on end. My husband jokes that I turn into a statue when I’m at my computer. I’ll just sit there, completely planted in my office chair, just staring blankly at the monitor. I don't bounce my leg, I don't tap my pen, nothing like that. \n\nAnd as for interrupting people or acting impulsively, definitely not. In those metrics meetings I was telling you about, I am almost always the quietest person in the room. I would never just talk over someone. If anything, I'm probably too quiet, mostly because, like I said, my mind has usually wandered off and I’m just scrambling internally to piece together what the conversation is even about so I don't sound completely out of the loop if someone actually does ask for my opinion. \n\nAnd making quick decisions? Oh, goodness, no. I am a chronic over-thinker. I mean, working in healthcare administration, you really can't make snap decisions anyway—everything has to go through compliance, or HR, or a committee review, so I'm very used to a slow process. But even in my personal life, I take absolutely forever to decide on anything. When we were doing that kitchen renovation I brought up earlier, it literally took me three entire weeks just to pick out the cabinet hardware. Three weeks! Because I had to compare every single brushed nickel drawer pull on the internet, reading reviews, ordering little samples to hold up against the wood... so no, impulsive is probably the very last word anyone would ever use to describe me. \n\nIt really is just the mental drift. It’s like my body is perfectly anchored to the desk, but my brain has completely left the building. Which is almost a double-edged sword, you know? Because my colleagues look at me sitting there so quietly and still, and they just assume I'm fully engaged and listening intently to every word of the presentation, when in reality I'm a million miles away thinking about grocery lists or an email I forgot to send yesterday. So, no, none of the fidgeting or impulsivity. Just the... the absolute wall with my focus.\n\n[Last Question]\nOutside of work, do you notice this same kind of mental drifting or trouble focusing affecting things at home—like following conversations with your husband, managing household tasks, or keeping track of personal appointments?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 50 / medical_or_health_services_manager / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"At work, my delays have started affecting clinic reporting deadlines and making other teams wait on me, and I’ve had to step back from leading parts of the volunteer health workshops because I can’t reliably keep the planning pieces moving.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, absolutely. It definitely affects things at home. My poor husband... I will be looking right at him while he's telling me about his day, maybe he's talking about something that happened with the car or asking what we should do for dinner, and suddenly I realize I haven't heard a single word he said for the last five minutes. I have to ask him to repeat himself constantly, which I know drives him crazy, even if he's too polite to say it. 

But honestly, while that's frustrating, it's the bigger responsibilities outside of just casual conversation that are really starting to fall apart on me, which is a huge part of why I finally made this appointment. Because it's not just forgetting a grocery list anymore. It's really causing major issues across the board. Like at work, for instance, my delays have actually started affecting our clinic reporting deadlines. It's just awful. I'm supposed to have these compliance and patient volume reports done by the third of every month, and lately, I'm just staring at the screen, drifting off, and I'm making other teams wait on me. The finance department, the clinical leads... they literally cannot do their jobs until I do mine, and I'm just bottlenecking the whole system because I can't stay focused long enough to finish a single spreadsheet. 

And it's not just my paid job, either. It's spilling over into my personal life, into the things I actually *love* doing outside of work. I’ve been involved in these volunteer health workshops in our community for years—we do these wonderful outreach programs, organizing free blood pressure screenings, diabetes education seminars, things like that. I usually help coordinate the whole thing. But recently? I actually had to step back from leading parts of the workshops. I had to call the committee chair last month and tell her I couldn't be a lead organizer this time around. I made some excuse about being too busy at the clinic, but the real truth, the honest truth, is that I just can’t reliably keep the planning pieces moving. There are just so many moving parts—booking the community center, coordinating the volunteer nurses, managing the email chains—and my brain just keeps drifting away from it all. I couldn't keep track of who I had contacted and who I hadn't, and I realized I was going to ruin the entire event if I stayed

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, sleep. You know, it's actually funny you ask about sleep, because so many of my friends—especially women my age, you know, hitting 50—are constantly complaining about insomnia or waking up at 3 AM and not being able to get back to sleep. But honestly? I sleep like an absolute rock. I really do. 

My husband is actually the one who struggles with his sleep. He has this whole elaborate routine now with a white noise machine and keeping the room at exactly 68 degrees, and he still tosses and turns half the night. But me? Once my head hits the pillow, I am completely out. We did buy a new mattress last year, one of those memory foam ones with the cooling gel layer, which I spent way too much time researching when I probably should have been doing something else, but it really is wonderful. I usually go to bed around 10:30, maybe 11:00 if we get caught up watching a show, and I sleep straight through until my alarm goes off at 6:00. I rarely even get up to use the restroom. 

And as for during the day... no, I wouldn't say I'm drowsy at all. I mean, I get that standard afternoon slump around 3 PM, like anyone working full-time in a clinic probably does, and maybe I'll grab a second cup of coffee from the breakroom, but it's never excessive. It's not like I'm struggling to keep my eyes open or falling asleep at my desk. When I'm sitting there staring blankly at my spreadsheets, like I was telling you about earlier, I am fully, one-hundred-percent awake. My eyes are wide open, I'm just... my brain is just somewhere else entirely. I'm not nodding off, I'm just completely distracted by whatever random thought has hijacked my attention. 

So, yeah, I feel very lucky in the sleep department. I'm getting a solid seven or eight hours of really restful sleep every single night without fail. I honestly wish my focus was half as reliable as my sleep schedule! So no, no insomnia or drowsiness to report there.

Subtypes: Childlike Silliness, Stupor, Catalepsy, Waxy Flexibility, Mutism, Negativism, Posturing, Mannerism, Stereotypy, Agitation, Grimacing, Echolalia, Echopraxia\n[S023] Negative_Symptoms: A state where emotions, motivation, speech, or social interest that should normally be present are diminished or lost.  Subtypes: Diminished Emotional Expression, Avolition, Alogia, Anhedonia, Asociality\n[S024] Depressed_Mood: A state where a sad, empty, or hopeless mood persists.  Subtypes: Depressed Mood, Tearfulness, Sadness, Emptiness, Emotional Numbness\n[S025] Loss_of_Interest: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.  Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.  Subtypes: Recurrent Thoughts of Death, Suicidal Ideation, Suicidal Impulse, Specific Plan for Suicide, Suicide Attempt\n[S083] Hopelessness: A feeling of despair or having no hope for the future.  Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions). Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.  Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame). Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health. Every single year, it was the exact same story. \"She's a bright girl, very capable, but she's just so careless.\" Or, \"She needs to apply herself more.\" \n\nAnd the homework... oh my goodness, the homework was a nightmare. My poor mother. She practically had to tether herself to the kitchen table with me every single night just to make sure I actually finished anything. If she didn't sit right there, I would just... I don't know, start doodling, or looking out the window, or I’d suddenly decide I needed to reorganize my entire pencil case. And that was if I even remembered to bring the homework home in the first place! Half the time I would leave my math book in my desk at school, or I’d lose the assignment sheet somewhere between the classroom and the bus. I was constantly losing track of things. So yes, it’s always been a struggle, it just feels like the stakes are so much higher now with my job, you know? Back then it was just a forgotten worksheet, but now it's patient data and clinic metrics, and I just can't afford to be that same careless little girl anymore.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate.   }

Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment. Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nYou know, it's funny you ask that, because my husband actually asked me something very similar just the other day. He was wondering if I was getting burned out, or maybe even depressed, because he sees how frustrated I get when I come home from the clinic. But honestly? No, not at all. I really don't feel sad or hopeless. \n\nI mean, don't get me wrong, I get stressed. Anyone in healthcare administration right now is stressed out of their minds. Just last week we had this huge unexpected compliance audit and I was running around trying to pull records, but that's just normal job stress, you know? It's not a general, lingering worry. I don't sit around feeling nervous about life or having dread about the future. I actually really love my life. My husband and I have a great setup, the kids are older and doing well, we actually just finished renovating our kitchen which has been a wonderful distraction... so there's really nothing for me to be genuinely down about. \n\nThe absolutely *only* time I get upset or anxious is when it is directly tied to me dropping the ball at work. Like when I realize I've spent three hours staring at that EPIC dashboard and got absolutely nothing done, *then* I get mad at myself. I get very frustrated, and I'll definitely vent to my husband about how incompetent I feel in those specific moments. But it's entirely tied to my inability to just do my work. Once I step away from the desk, or once it's the weekend, I'm perfectly fine. It's not this dark cloud following me around, and it's not a generalized panic. It's just... sheer, utter annoyance at my own brain, if that makes sense? Because I know I am smart enough to do the job, I just can't seem to make my brain cooperate. So, yeah, no persistent sadness or anything like that. Just the focus thing.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.  Subtypes: Recurrent Thoughts of Death, Suicidal Ideation, Suicidal Impulse, Specific Plan for Suicide, Suicide Attempt\n[S083] Hopelessness: A feeling of despair or having no hope for the future.  Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions). Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.  Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities. Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nNo, not really. I mean, I don't... I really don't have that physical restlessness at all. Actually, it’s quite the opposite. I can sit perfectly still for hours on end. My husband jokes that I turn into a statue when I’m at my computer. I’ll just sit there, completely planted in my office chair, just staring blankly at the monitor. I don't bounce my leg, I don't tap my pen, nothing like that. \n\nAnd as for interrupting people or acting impulsively, definitely not. In those metrics meetings I was telling you about, I am almost always the quietest person in the room. I would never just talk over someone. If anything, I'm probably too quiet, mostly because, like I said, my mind has usually wandered off and I’m just scrambling internally to piece together what the conversation is even about so I don't sound completely out of the loop if someone actually does ask for my opinion. \n\nAnd making quick decisions? Oh, goodness, no. I am a chronic over-thinker. I mean, working in healthcare administration, you really can't make snap decisions anyway—everything has to go through compliance, or HR, or a committee review, so I'm very used to a slow process. But even in my personal life, I take absolutely forever to decide on anything. When we were doing that kitchen renovation I brought up earlier, it literally took me three entire weeks just to pick out the cabinet hardware. Three weeks! Because I had to compare every single brushed nickel drawer pull on the internet, reading reviews, ordering little samples to hold up against the wood... so no, impulsive is probably the very last word anyone would ever use to describe me. \n\nIt really is just the mental drift. It’s like my body is perfectly anchored to the desk, but my brain has completely left the building. Which is almost a double-edged sword, you know? Because my colleagues look at me sitting there so quietly and still, and they just assume I'm fully engaged and listening intently to every word of the presentation, when in reality I'm a million miles away thinking about grocery lists or an email I forgot to send yesterday. So, no, none of the fidgeting or impulsivity. Just the... the absolute wall with my focus.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate.
